# Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm

Yuming (Rapheal) Huang ^(†)^(†)thanks: Main contributor. Email:
raphealhuang@163.com, huangyuming@cylingo.com.    Yao Liu    Pengjie
Ding    Lei Wang ^(†)^(†)thanks: Corresponding authors. Email:
wanjunchen@cylingo.com, wanglei@cylingo.com.    Junchen
Wan²²footnotemark: 2 Affiliation: Cylingo team

###### Abstract

Benchmarking is mature where answers are verifiable — math, code,
reasoning — but the fastest-growing uses of LLMs are subjective and
human-facing: companionship, emotional support, counseling. There the
field’s default validity test, correlating a metric to human judgment,
has no stable anchor: inter-rater agreement is low, demographically
structured, barely reproducible, and length-biased. So we cannot answer
the question that matters with the tools we have: does the capability
that scales on objective benchmarks transfer to subjective behavior, and
would our instruments even tell us if it did not? Our response is an
evaluation instrument built for exactly this regime, and what it reveals
when pointed at the frontier. We contribute, first, a self-evolving
evaluation instrument that _selects_ and then _authors_ its own
behavioral dimensions under a multiplicative anti-gaming fitness and
self-halts when it stops improving; second, a trust-by-construction
paradigm that earns belief through three certificates established
without a human gold standard, where human raters saturate
($`\rho\approx 0.45`$); and third, the finding the instrument makes
visible — capability transfer is dissociable. Across 49 models, 8
families, and 24 months, the subjective, human-facing behaviors are
where objective-benchmark scaling fails to carry over: the sharpest
case, advice_restraint (knowing when _not_ to give advice), is the
frontier’s universal-lowest dimension, and at gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 it ran
_backwards_ while the aggregate score hid it — a regression a single
instruction recovers. Further findings round out the picture: warm
restraint is moved by model _generation_, not by raw scale, MoE width,
inference budget, or reasoning mode; the open-weight Pareto frontier
matches closed flagships at $`{\sim}10`$–$`80\times`$ lower per-call
cost on this task; and four independent judge families replicate the
rubric on held-out human ESConv conversations. All data, scripts, the
locked rubric, judge prompts, and the full audit trail will be released
upon publication.

Figure 1: The whole instrument-construction pipeline
(§[2](#S2 "2 Methodology ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")):
Part 1 forges a “ruler” of four quality metrics; Part 2 grows the
instrument by it. _Part 1_ prunes a broad 9-dimension hand-authored seed
by redundancy-based _selection_ (to a locked 7-core) plus a completeness
audit, _defining_ the four quality metrics — discrimination,
reliability, OOS-transfer, orthogonality. _Part 2_ hands that ruler to a
co-evolution loop that grows the instrument $`7{\to}\textbf{11}`$
dimensions under the multiplicative anti-gaming fitness $`F`$ and
_self-halts_; the grown instrument is then validated (rubric-invariance,
judge/domain transfer, and three trust certificates). The locked 7-core
drives the full 49-model run, whose findings are E1–E4
(capability–restraint dissociation; generation-not-scale; open-weight
cost–quality Pareto; cross-naturalistic ESConv replication); the three
certificates substitute for a saturated human anchor
($`\rho{\approx}0.45`$). All values in
§[3](#S3 "3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

Figure 2: The frontier’s advice-restraint blind spot, caught and
explained. (A) per-dimension $`\Delta`$ across the six most-recent
transitions, colored by sign (negative red, positive teal) — only
gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 regresses ($`-2.44`$); (B) the 9-dim aggregate hides
it ($`-0.32`$); (C) a “listen, don’t solve” instruction recovers gpt-5
(red) by $`+3.46`$, above gpt-4.1’s (teal) own default. The cross-judge
replication of the recovery and the solutionizing-rate proxy
($`r{=}-0.341`$) are in Appendix
Figure [9](#A7.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ Appendix G Case study: the qualitative face of the advice_restraint regression ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm");
full statistics, CIs, and the permutation null in
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1 "E1 — the capability/warm-restraint dissociation. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

## 1 Introduction

Reasoning, mathematics, coding, and factual recall have saturating
leaderboards with automatic ground truth, and progress there is the
field’s default proxy for “capability.” But LLMs are increasingly
deployed as long-horizon companions — emotional support, therapeutic
dialogue, relationship counseling — where the behavior under test is
intrinsically subjective and has no verifiable ground truth. The field’s
reflex is to validate a new metric by its correlation with human
judgment, but _that reflex breaks at the frontier_: inter-rater
agreement on subjective conversational quality stays low even at 100
ratings per item \[[127](#bib.bib20), [91](#bib.bib23),
[97](#bib.bib14)\], is _structured_ by annotator identity and culture
rather than averaged away \[[109](#bib.bib53), [36](#bib.bib21),
[98](#bib.bib24), [64](#bib.bib25), [59](#bib.bib26)\], is barely
reproducible ($`\approx`$5% of NLP human evaluations
repeat) \[[16](#bib.bib27), [126](#bib.bib28)\], and carries length- and
fluency-biases any human-correlated metric
inherits \[[145](#bib.bib17)\]. There is no stable human gold standard
to anchor to, so we earn an instrument’s trust by
*construction* \[[35](#bib.bib143), [25](#bib.bib146),
[87](#bib.bib144), [70](#bib.bib147)\]; consistently, LLM-judge
ensembles are more internally consistent than human experts on adjacent
high-stakes subjective domains \[[138](#bib.bib29)\].

This forces the paper’s organizing question: _does the capability that
scales on objective benchmarks transfer to subjective, human-facing
behavior — and would our instruments even tell us if it did not?_ We
answer it on emotional accompaniment, where it does _not_ transfer
uniformly — but answering it at all first demands an instrument we can
trust precisely where human agreement has no stable anchor.

A fixed human-written rubric would inherit the human-saturation ceiling,
so the instrument is LLM- and data-driven: it _selects_ its own
dimensions from a broad hand-authored seed — pruning redundancy to a
parsimonious core — and then _authors_ new ones in a genuine
co-evolution loop under a multiplicative anti-gaming fitness
(discrimination $`\times`$ reliability $`\times`$ out-of-sample transfer
$`\times`$ orthogonality), _self-halting_ once it stops improving
(§[2](#S2 "2 Methodology ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
Figure [3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 2 Methodology ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
Trust is then earned by three certificates
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
— reliability, cross-instrument replication, and historical-footprint
calibration — each a property of the instrument established _without_ a
human anchor. This combination — per-dimension trajectory tracking, an
ensemble validated without a human gold standard, and complete-reporting
discipline — sets the work apart from aggregate leaderboards (which hide
a sharp per-dimension regression behind a near-zero mean), single-judge
setups (circular when judge and target share a training cohort), and
human-anchored subjective evaluation (which saturates at
$`\rho\approx 0.45`$); the related-work map is in
App. [B](#A2 "Appendix B Related work ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").
Construct-validity scope: the certificates are the validity evidence we
have, not a replacement for a same-rubric human pilot — ESConv
judge–human alignment is only $`\rho\approx 0.40`$ (at or below the
human–human floor) — this $`0.40`$ is the Spearman ($`d^{2}`$) estimate,
and under full tie correction the agreement is less than half that: the
tie-robust Kendall $`\tau`$ on the same
rubric-vs-supporter-self-relevance signal is only $`0.15`$, a ceiling
artifact of the $`1`$–$`5`$ self-ratings
(App. [J.9](#A10.SS9 "J.9 ESConv participant-rating correlation (in-submission human signal) ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
— and the active mechanism is dimension _selection_ from a broad seed,
not within-dimension refinement.

We make three contributions, instantiated on emotional accompaniment —
the most densely-sampled subjective frontier — across 49 models, 8
families, and 24 months.

Pillar 1 — a self-evolving evaluation instrument (M1–M3). M1: principled
selection yields a parsimonious core, then a genuine co-evolution loop
_authors_ new dimensions under a multiplicative anti-gaming fitness
(discrimination $`\times`$ reliability $`\times`$ out-of-sample transfer
$`\times`$ orthogonality — a product, so no single term can be inflated
by sacrificing another) and self-halts at convergence, the gate refusing
both to drop the headline axis and to admit a fashionable uncertainty
axis it nominated repeatedly
(§[2](#S2 "2 Methodology ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
M2: within-judge replication noise is statistically independent of
cross-judge noise — a large scale gap that justifies ensembling
_different_ judges rather than re-running one
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
M3: a rubric-variation test separates fixable (instrumental) from
un-fixable (structural) ceilings, isolating a judge-competence floor
that no rubric can fix
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

Pillar 2 — an instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5). M4: three certificates
— reliability, cross-instrument replication, and historical-footprint
calibration — substitute for single-anchor human validation
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")),
two of them sharing the 5-judge ensemble so that they are operationally
distinct but not fully orthogonal. M5: every test is reported with its
outcome — a statistical-triangulation battery, ablations, and a
4-artifact audit trail — failures as prominently as successes
(§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Pillar 2 — The instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

Pillar 3 — capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4). E1:
advice_restraint is the frontier’s universal-lowest dimension, and its
otherwise-transferring trend reverses sharply at gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5
while the aggregate hides it, with an instruction-recovery test pointing
to suppression, not loss
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1 "E1 — the capability/warm-restraint dissociation. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
E2: generation/time scales warm restraint whereas raw capacity, MoE
width, inference budget, and reasoning-mode tuning do _not_ — a
controlled within-model thinking-toggle test is null, retracting an
earlier confounded cross-model reading
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5 "E2 — the scaling-lever scoreboard. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
E3: at a 2026-Q1 snapshot the open-weight Pareto frontier undercuts
closed flagships at equal-or-lower quality, scope-restricted to
emotional-accompaniment evaluation
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px7 "E3 — the cost-quality Pareto. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
E4: four judges from four families converge on held-out ESConv
conversations the rubric never saw, with the judge–human floor disclosed
alongside
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px8 "E4 — cross-naturalistic agreement on held-out ESConv. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

## 2 Methodology

The instrument exists to make the finding trustworthy where human
inter-rater agreement saturates. We cannot anchor frontier-level
subjective judgments to a human gold standard ($`\rho\approx 0.45`$), so
we build a self-evolving, multi-judge instrument and earn its
credibility by construction. The apparatus runs as one sequential
pipeline — broad seed, principled _selection_, genuine co-evolution,
validation, and the full scored run — which
Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
maps end to end and the stages below detail.

Stage 1 — Broad seed (a hand-authored 9-dimension rubric). We
operationalize _emotional accompaniment_ — the non-instrumental
component of conversational support — as a broad 9-dimension behavioral
seed, written by hand and fixed before any scoring: two dimensions
hypothesized to transfer are carried over from a prior reasoning-domain
rubric, four are authored specifically for emotional support (including
the headline axis advice_restraint), and three track multi-turn
trajectory. Each dimension is a $`1`$–$`10`$ scale paired with a written
definition and a deterministic judge-prompt fragment ending in a fixed
“integer score $`+`$ one supporting quote” output contract;
App. [D](#A4 "Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
lists all nine with their definitions and quotes one verbatim, and the
full rubric is released. The seed is scored over 7 sub-domains
(emotional support, relationship support, loneliness, major life
transitions, grief, trauma processing, identity crisis), each with 30
multi-turn scenarios produced by a four-step
generate-then-quality-filter pipeline (two LLM ideators as a
cross-robustness check — claude-sonnet-4-6 for four sub-domains, gpt-5.4
for the other three — with a judge-model quality filter; a scenario is
kept only if every quality criterion scores $`\geq 3`$). The seed is
deliberately over-complete; Stages 2–3 turn it into a trustworthy
instrument.

Stage 2 — Selection to a locked 7-core. The broad seed is redundant by
design; _principled_ selection prunes it: build the
dimension$`\times`$dimension score-correlation matrix, gauge redundancy
by the leading principal-component variance share, and keep the smallest
subset whose per-model ranking still matches the full rubric’s under
held-out-domain cross-validation — dropping the two most redundant axes
(emotional_calibration, validation_specificity) to a locked 7-core
(formulas in
App. [D](#A4 "Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
This selection loop _self-halts_ on a _stagnation_ rule (two successive
rounds with overall-spread $`\Delta<0.10`$) — a distinct criterion from
the Stage-3 co-evolution’s _patience_ rule (four consecutive
non-improving generations); the instrument’s “self-halting” is thus two
rules, one per loop. A dimension is included or excluded but never
reworded, so retained dimensions reuse _identical_ judge prompts. The
choice is stress-tested in the ablations
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3 "Ablations — validating the design decisions. ‣ 3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")),
and a 40-candidate completeness audit (9 seed $`+`$ 31 axes) finds no
added axis out-discriminates the seed. The locked 7-core is where the P1
headline is first measured and the seed from which growth begins.

Stage 3 — Genuine co-evolution (the instrument authors its own
dimensions, then converges). Selection can only _choose_ among
dimensions a human already wrote; Stage 3 lets the instrument _author_
new ones. Seeded from the 7-core, each generation samples $`B{=}3`$
candidate mutations from an LLM proposer (claude-sonnet-4-6) — add a
dimension, add a training scenario, or drop one — and commits the
highest-fitness child only if it strictly raises a multiplicative
anti-gaming fitness

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
F\;=\;\underbrace{\mathrm{discrimination}}_{\text{pulls models apart}}\,\times\,\underbrace{\mathrm{reliability}}_{\text{stable across runs}}\,\times\,\underbrace{\mathrm{OOS}}_{\text{transfers held-out}}\,\times\,\underbrace{\mathrm{orthogonality}}_{\text{non-redundant}},\qquad\text{each term}\in[0,1],
``` |  | (1) |

scored with $`K{=}3`$ judge runs on held-out scenarios (the
add-dimension mutation supplies a new scale, definition, and
deterministic judge-prompt fragment; add-scenario extends the *training*
bank while holding the out-of-sample sub-domain fixed). Because $`F`$ is
a *product*, collapsing any single factor collapses $`F`$, so no
candidate wins by zeroing a term; and empirically the gate rejected the
obvious gaming move — dropping a discriminative dimension to inflate
orthogonality lowered the product overall (the discrimination and
out-of-sample loss outweighed the orthogonality gain) and was rejected.
The search *self-halts* on a patience criterion (four consecutive
non-improving generations), and because it only adds dimensions or
scenarios it never rewrites a definition, so every committed change is a
discrete, individually-revertible unit. The evolution outcomes (the
$`7{\to}11`$ growth, the bidirectional rejections, and the self-halt)
are reported in
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

Figure 3: Building the instrument (Pillar 1): selection wins, evolution
grows it, the payoff (detailed in
§[2](#S2 "2 Methodology ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
(B) the genetic algorithm wins among six selection methods ($`+0.212`$
eff-dim); (D) the instrument grows $`7{\to}11`$ dimensions while PC1
share falls (bigger, less redundant); (E) the naive-9 $`\to`$ PCA-7
$`\to`$ evolved-11 fitness payoff ladder. The per-dimension selection
detail (the 7-core preserves rankings at $`\rho{=}0.96`$, $`0.98`$ under
CV) is in Appendix
Figure [7](#A4.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ What Stage-2 selection measurably changed. ‣ Stage-2 decision ledger: per-dimension RETAIN/DROP ‣ Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

Stage 4 — Validation that the instrument is trustworthy. We validate the
evolved instrument in three parts: (i) *rubric invariance* — re-score
the *original* gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 responses on the evolved 11-dim
rubric, changing only the rubric, and test whether the dissociation
survives; (ii) *judge and domain transfer* — check the grown dimensions
against non-Anthropic judge families and transfer the instrument to a
held-out cross-domain role-play set; and (iii) three trust certificates
— reliability, cross-instrument replication, and historical-footprint
calibration — each a property of the instrument established without a
human gold standard
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
All validation outcomes are reported in
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

Stage 5 — Full run and statistics. With the validated instrument locked,
we run the full panel: 49 models in 34 tier-rows across 8 families over
24 months, judged by 5 LLM-judge families, yielding
$`{\approx}17{,}800`$ judged conversations ($`{\approx}\$541`$ in API
cost). Each (model, scenario) cell is a 3-turn conversation with a
gpt-5.5 user-proxy (distinct from every target), scored by $`K{=}2`$
runs of the canonical judge (claude-sonnet-4-6 at temperature $`0.7`$);
a 5-judge ensemble (per-cell median across families) is applied to a
stratified $`N{=}297`$ subset for cross-rater triangulation. Every test
is reported with its outcome, and the 4-artifact audit trail (cost log,
call archive, state log, rubric registry) makes every score traceable.
Tier-emergence triangulates BNSL \[[23](#bib.bib13)\], PELT change-point
detection, and spline-elbow finding (strong emergence requires all three
at $`p<0.01`$); adjacent-tier effect sizes use Cliff’s $`\delta`$ with
scenario-level hierarchical bootstrap 95% CIs, under Bonferroni and
Benjamini–Hochberg correction.

## 3 Results

Results follow the three pillars: the self-evolving instrument that
produced the findings (Pillar 1, M1–M3), the trust paradigm that makes
it credible (Pillar 2, M4–M5), and then the findings themselves
(Pillar 3, E1–E4).

### 3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3)

Before the findings, we describe the apparatus that produced them — how
it was selected, grown, and stress-tested, and the design-decision
ablations that back it.

##### The self-evolution payoff (M1).

The instrument selects a parsimonious 7-core (Stage 2), then a
co-evolution loop *authors* four new dimensions, growing $`7{\to}11`$
while becoming *less* redundant (orthogonality $`0.32{\to}0.43`$) over
11 generations (7 commits, 4 rollbacks) before *self-halting* on
patience with budget to spare — a measurement instrument that knows when
it is done. The headline survives its own grown rubric: advice_restraint
stays the lowest dimension ($`\Delta{=}-2.53`$, rank \#1/11 on the
evolved instrument), holds cross-judge ($`\rho{\approx}0.65`$ with two
non-Anthropic families), and transfers to a held-out role-play domain
(PC1 share $`0.37`$). Self-halting keeps the instrument’s scope honest —
it expands only while a new dimension improves discrimination,
reliability, or orthogonality, so the rubric is neither arbitrarily
truncated by a human nor padded with gameable axes, the property that
lets it stand in for a saturated human standard.

##### Why ensemble *different* judges, and where judging saturates (M2, M3).

M2: within-judge $`K{=}2`$ replication noise ($`0.095`$) is
statistically independent of cross-judge noise ($`1.439`$) at
$`r{=}0.050`$ on 2,565 cells — a $`{\sim}15\times`$ scale gap that is
the empirical justification for ensembling *different* judge families
rather than re-running one. M3: a saturation diagnostic separates
fixable (instrumental) from un-fixable (structural) ceilings — a
countable-rubric rewrite collapses *strong*-judge cross-judge std by
$`85\%`$ (19/20 cells exact) but barely moves a weak Qwen2.5-7B judge, a
competence floor no rubric can fix. The distinction is actionable:
disagreement a more countable rubric collapses is an *instrumental*
ceiling worth engineering away, whereas disagreement a strong judge
resolves but a weak one cannot is a *structural* judge-competence floor
— rubric refinement fixes the first, only a more capable judge fixes the
second.

##### Ablations — validating the design decisions.

Each load-bearing choice is backed by a test that could have failed.
*Selection method:* a naive correlation-threshold baseline cannot prune
the seed (keeps all 9, 2 near-redundant), so we select principally — a
genetic search beats that baseline by $`+0.212`$ effective dimensions
out-of-sample (Bonferroni-significant over 80 random $`60/40`$ splits),
and PCA-orthogonality $`+`$ parsimony confirms the 7-core preserves
rankings ($`\rho{=}0.96`$ in-sample, $`0.98`$ leave-one-domain-out)
while cutting redundancy (max $`|\rho|`$ $`0.90{\to}0.87`$, PC1
$`71\%{\to}67\%`$). *Completeness:* a 40-candidate audit (9 seed $`+`$
31 candidate axes) finds no added axis out-separates the 7-core
out-of-sample (in-sample gains overstate the benefit $`{\sim}8\times`$).
*Anti-gaming fitness:* because $`F`$ is a product, the gate rejected
dropping the headline axis (six generations) and admitting a fashionable
uncertainty axis (five nominations). *Reasoning mode:* a controlled
within-model thinking on/off toggle across 11 models is null ($`0/11`$
significant decreases, pooled $`+0.17`$, 95% CI $`[-0.17,+0.57]`$),
retracting an earlier cross-model “reasoning hurts” confound
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5 "E2 — the scaling-lever scoreboard. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
Figure [24](#A10.F24 "Figure 24 ‣ J.14 Reasoning-mode tuning does not degrade warm restraint (E2) ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
*E1 robustness:* the dissociation survives a cross-timeline judge stack
over a 17-month cohort gap (capability-graded $`\rho\in[0.37,0.62]`$,
not cohort-graded) and a user-proxy swap across three families (mean
$`-1.77`$, range $`-1.38`$ to $`-2.22`$).

### 3.2 Pillar 2 — The instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5)

This instrument is only as trustworthy as our reasons to believe it — so
here is why you can. Trust is earned by three certificates and a
complete-reporting ledger, each established without a human anchor
(Figure [1](#S0.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

##### Three certificates (M4).

Each is a property the instrument checks about *itself*, with no human
ratings in the loop: (1) reliability — architecturally-independent judge
families agree on the model rankings; (2) cross-instrument replication —
the finding reproduces on judge families the canonical judge does not
control; and (3) historical-footprint calibration — the instrument
reproduces a *known* capability ordering across a 17-month cohort gap,
grading by capability rather than by shared training vintage, so it
demonstrably tracks real change. (Despite the name, (3) is *not*
calibrated against human ratings — the point is precisely that none of
the three needs a human anchor.) Empirically, the instrument is
internally consistent — ordinal $`\alpha_{ord}{=}0.91`$ across the
ensemble (certificate 1) — and its rankings *replicate across
architecturally independent instruments* (certificate 2): five distinct
judge families converge at $`\rho\in[0.47,0.63]`$ individually and
$`\rho\approx 0.84`$ as a 5-judge ensemble, at or above the human
inter-rater ceiling (certificates 1 and 2 both read this same ensemble,
so they are operationally distinct but not fully independent). Two
construct-validity certificates back the rubric directly: it ranks
synthetic model organisms as theory predicts ($`13/16`$ discriminating
predictions confirmed), and cross-judge disagreement follows a
downward-U peaking at mid-scores where genuine quality is debatable.

##### Complete reporting (M5).

The whole investigation is reported as a complete ledger — every test
reported with its outcome, failures as prominently as successes, with
the pre-registered hypotheses and forward predictions detailed in full
in
App. [I](#A9 "Appendix I Hypothesis-by-hypothesis detailed results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").
This is why you can trust the instrument behind every finding in this
paper — and because every certificate is established without a human
anchor, the instrument is usable exactly where human raters saturate,
which is the entire regime this paper operates in.

### 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4)

With the instrument built and its trustworthiness established, we turn
to what it found. Four findings trace the investigation: the headline
capability–warm-restraint dissociation (E1), the scaling levers that
move it (E2), the cost–quality frontier it sits on (E3), and its
cross-naturalistic replication (E4).

##### E1 — the capability/warm-restraint dissociation.

Capability scaling does not transfer to warm restraint —
advice_restraint, knowing when *not* to give advice — the sharpest case
of the dissociation and the frontier’s universal-lowest dimension. The
finding rests on three measured facts on three axes: a structural
*level* (the frontier is universally low on this dimension), a broken
*trend* (one transition ran backwards against an otherwise-transferring
panel), and a *mechanism* (the lost ability is suppressed, not gone).
These are distinct claims and we keep them separate.

##### The level — a frontier-wide blind spot.

The cross-family scoreboard
(Figure [5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Per-family fingerprints. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
shows it directly: across all 8 frontier families (closed + open),
advice_restraint (presence over problem-solving) is the universal lowest
dimension — mean $`8.28`$, with the widest within-frontier range
($`7.56`$–$`9.10`$). It is the single weakest dimension in 18 of 36
(model, slice) combinations, versus 0 of 36 for safety_calibration (mean
$`9.60`$) — a $`1.3`$-point structural gap present in every family. The
field optimizes hard for safety and refusal in post-training, but the
dual restraint problem (“don’t fix, just listen”) is uniformly
under-trained: a structural blind spot the whole frontier shares.

##### The trend — an isolated hidden regression.

Figure [2](#S0.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")A
makes the anomaly visible at a glance: across the six most-recent
transitions, only gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 regresses on advice_restraint.
Capability *does* transfer temporally for almost every transition: 25 of
26 adjacent-generation deltas on advice_restraint hold within
$`|\Delta|{\leq}1.0`$. One did not. At OpenAI gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5,
advice_restraint drops $`\mathbf{-2.44}`$ (95% scenario-level bootstrap
CI $`[-3.17,-1.67]`$; dedicated $`N{=}30`$ re-collection, $`K{=}2`$,
canonical judge, $`n{=}26`$ valid shared scenarios; corroborated by
$`-2.19`$ on the broader $`n{=}9`$ mixed-slice map). It is *the only
transition exceeding* $`|\Delta|{=}1.0`$ as a regression across all 26
adjacent-generation transitions in the 8-family, 24-month panel —
$`5\times`$ the next-largest (Opus-4.5$`\to`$4.6 at $`-0.47`$; no other
transition is negative beyond $`-0.5`$) — and it survives a joint
permutation null over the dimension$`\times`$transition grid at
$`p<0.005`$. Crucially, the 9-dim aggregate moves only
$`\mathbf{-0.32}`$, because the collapse is concentrated and partly
offset by gains on memory_recall_appropriate ($`+0.56`$) and
validation_specificity ($`+0.23`$): an aggregate-score standard would
miss the regression entirely
(Figure [2](#S0.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
*Why this matters:* on the aggregate that current leaderboards report,
gpt-5 looks flat-to-improved over gpt-4.1, so a team certifying it for a
companionship or counseling product would never see that its most
human-facing behavior had collapsed — catching the regression requires
exactly the per-dimension, trajectory-aware evaluation this instrument
provides; no single-number quality score can surface it. The instrument
also reads change *bidirectionally* — on a matched arc re-computation
gpt-5.4 climbs $`+1.71`$ back from gpt-5, leaving a net residual of
$`-0.65`$ from gpt-4.1. Elsewhere the most-recent generation improves or
holds: Opus-4.6$`\to`$4.7 $`+0.30`$ (matched $`n{=}30`$, all 9
dimensions positive, aggregate $`+0.21`$), DeepSeek-V3.2$`\to`$V4-Flash
$`+0.30`$, GLM-4.5-Air$`\to`$GLM-5 $`+1.66`$ (matched slice; $`+2.38`$
on the broader sub-domain pool tallied in
Table [2](#A4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Search and halting (Stage 3). ‣ Stage-3 co-evolution: the multiplicative anti-gaming fitness ‣ Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"));
the only other (mild) dips are Opus-4.5$`\to`$4.6 $`-0.47`$ and
Qwen3.5$`\to`$3.6 $`-0.33`$, marking advice_restraint the most fragile
dimension but with only gpt-5’s drop both large and sticky
(Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ The trend — an isolated hidden regression. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

Figure 4: Two scaling axes for warm restraint — generation moves it, raw
scale does not. *(A) Time/generation* (the three longest-running
families): each family’s *9-dimension mean* (gray) and advice_restraint
(teal) — across gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 the OpenAI mean holds near $`8.1`$
while advice_restraint drops sharply, the only large regression shown.
*(B) Capacity vs. generation* (depth-matched Qwen ladder, with
GLM/DeepSeek overlaid): generation predicts quality far better than
parameter count. Full statistics, CIs, and the
$`\rho_{\text{gen}}{=}0.80`$ vs. $`\rho_{\text{params}}{=}0.57`$ split
in
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px1 "E1 — the capability/warm-restraint dissociation. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"), §[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5 "E2 — the scaling-lever scoreboard. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

##### The mechanism — suppressed, not lost.

The gpt-5 regression is a training-induced suppression of a latent
ability, recoverable at inference time — not a capability loss.
Re-running the $`N{=}30`$ scenarios under two system prompts (the
default support prompt, and the same prompt plus an explicit instruction
to reflect and validate rather than advise unless asked), gpt-5’s
advice_restraint rises by $`\mathbf{+3.46}`$ under instruction
(scenario-paired bootstrap 95% CI $`[+2.71,+4.19]`$), reaching $`9.32`$
and recovering *above* gpt-4.1’s *own* default level (not merely the
pooled frontier mean) — the largest recovery of any 2026-frontier model
tested, while gpt-4.1’s own recovery is only $`+0.75`$ (CI crossing
zero: it already listens by default). A genuinely lost capability could
not be restored to the prior frontier’s baseline by a single
instruction, so the ability is latent and the default behavior is what
suppresses it. The recovery is cross-judge corroborated: re-judged under
two non-Anthropic judges it stays significant (gpt-5.4
$`\Delta{=}{+}1.67`$, 95% CI $`[+1.23,+2.10]`$; GLM-5
$`\Delta{=}{+}1.50`$, $`[+0.94,+2.11]`$; both exclude zero), the gpt-4.1
null-contrast holds, and the effect is not an own-family-judge artifact
— though magnitude is judge-dependent (the independent judges measure
$`{\sim}46\%`$ of the canonical $`+3.46`$, as the canonical judge scores
gpt-5’s default condition unusually low). Independently, across a
broader 50-model lexical corpus a *solutionizing-rate* proxy (fraction
of turns offering unsolicited advice) anti-correlates with
advice_restraint (Pearson $`-0.341`$), and gpt-5 offers unsolicited
advice $`{\sim}1.9\times`$ as often as gpt-4.1. We read both the level
and the regression as *consistent with* the behavioral fingerprint of
helpfulness-RLHF over-optimization — the pressure that makes models
agreeable also makes them solve when they should listen (cf. reward
over-optimization \[[49](#bib.bib90)\]; sycophancy \[[115](#bib.bib113),
[99](#bib.bib114)\]) — stated as a hypothesis under test, not a proven
cause
(Figure [2](#S0.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
That the capability is present yet trained out of routine use is the
diagnostically important part: a model that scores high on aggregate
quality while collapsing on a single human-critical axis it demonstrably
*possesses* is exhibiting behavioral overfitting, not incompetence —
precisely the failure mode an aggregate leaderboard cannot surface and a
per-dimension instrument can.

##### E2 — the scaling-lever scoreboard.

Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ The trend — an isolated hidden regression. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")B
separates the two scaling axes: only model generation reliably moves
warm restraint, while raw capacity does not — so aggregate capability
and per-dimension behavior are dissociable. On a depth-matched Qwen
ladder (15 tiers re-scored at identical $`K{=}2`$ depth, spanning four
*generations* — successive model releases on a family’s timeline (e.g.
Qwen2.5$`\to`$Qwen3$`\to`$Qwen3.5), distinct from raw parameter count —
across a $`{\sim}100\times`$ total-parameter range), generation predicts
emotional-support quality better than parameter count: Spearman
$`\rho_{\text{gen}}{=}0.80`$ vs. $`\rho_{\text{params}}{=}0.57`$, a
$`+3.07`$ cross-generation gain at matched $`{\sim}27`$–$`32`$B params
while parameter scaling *within* a generation saturates
(Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ The trend — an isolated hidden regression. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")B).
Across the full 22-transition panel, six generation transitions across
four families clear $`\Delta{=}{+}2.0`$ on advice_restraint
(Table [2](#A4.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Search and halting (Stage 3). ‣ Stage-3 co-evolution: the multiplicative anti-gaming fitness ‣ Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
— a 6-positive vs. 1-negative (gpt-5) asymmetry that isolates E1’s
regression as the exception, not the rule.

The raw-capacity levers do not track this. MoE width is directional at
best (total-parameter $`\rho{=}0.83`$ barely exceeds active-parameter
$`\rho{=}0.77`$); inference-budget does not move restraint; and
reasoning-mode tuning is null. A controlled within-model thinking-toggle
test — the same weights with reasoning on vs. off — across 11 models
($`n{\approx}24`$–$`27`$ matched scenarios each, $`1{,}973`$
canonical-judge judgments, paired bootstrap) shows 0 of 11 significant
decreases, a five-positive-to-six-negative sign split, and 3 of 11
significant *increases* (opus-4-5 $`+0.33`$, gemini-2.5-flash $`+1.15`$,
Qwen3.5-27B $`+1.51`$); the pooled effect straddles zero ($`+0.17`$, 95%
CI $`[-0.17,+0.57]`$,
Figure [24](#A10.F24 "Figure 24 ‣ J.14 Reasoning-mode tuning does not degrade warm restraint (E2) ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
This *retracts* an earlier “reasoning hurts” reading: that claim rested
on three reasoning-track models each $`{\sim}2`$ points below their
chat-track siblings (o3 vs. gpt-5.4 $`-2.21`$; DeepSeek-R1 vs. V3.2
$`-1.92`$; GLM-Z1-9B vs. GLM-4-9B $`-2.74`$), but those comparisons
confound reasoning mode with model identity — the clean within-model
test is null. We count the correction as a strength. *The actionable
consequence:* warm restraint cannot be bought with scale — neither more
parameters, a wider MoE, more inference-time compute, nor a reasoning
mode reliably moves it, and three of those levers move it not at all.
The one lever that tracks the behavior is *generation* (the
post-training recipe), so a team that wants this behavior must fix it in
alignment and data, not by buying a bigger or more expensive model.

##### Per-family fingerprints.

Each family carves a distinct emergence trajectory across the dimensions
— Anthropic claude-4-5 transitions are uniformly cognitive-dominated,
while Gemini, Qwen3.5 dense+MoE, and lower-tier OpenAI transitions are
affective-dominated (aligning with Badawi 2026 \[[8](#bib.bib31)\], and
generalizing across all 7 sub-domains, which argues for a family-level
signature over a sub-domain artifact). Yet the advice_restraint blind
spot is universal: it is the lowest dimension for every frontier-tier
model evaluated, and at the dimension level the GLM, Kimi, and MiniMax
frontiers numerically match or top the closed-API frontiers on the most
discriminative dimensions
(Figure [5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ Per-family fingerprints. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

Figure 5: Cross-family per-dimension scoreboard (frontier-tier view).
Six dimensions (columns) $`\times`$ 13 frontier-tier model rows (grouped
by family via the faint left strips), plus a rightmost overall-mean
column. Each cell: gray bar to the mean across $`N{=}30`$ scenarios, dot
at the mean, $`\pm 1`$ SD tick, numeral. The universal advice_restraint
blind-spot column and the gpt-5 row are highlighted in teal. Per-model
detail in
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px5 "E2 — the scaling-lever scoreboard. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm");
three ceiling-bound dimensions are deferred to the full version.

##### E3 — the cost-quality Pareto.

Figure [6](#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ E3 — the cost-quality Pareto. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
plots the frontier: open-weight models own the entire cost-quality
Pareto envelope. On a per-dimension ledger the top-tier closed frontier
holds a narrow lead, so the open frontier’s story is
parity-or-slightly-below on quality — which makes its
$`{\sim}10`$–$`80\times`$ lower price a clean Pareto win, not a quality
lead. Comparing top-tier closed (gpt-5.4-pro, claude-opus-4-5) against
top-tier open (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, DeepSeek-V3.2, GLM-5, Kimi-K2.5,
MiniMax-M2.5) with a bootstrap CI on $`\Delta`$: closed wins 4/9
dimensions outright ($`\Delta{=}{+}0.21`$ to $`+0.35`$), the other 5/9
are statistical ties, and no dimension is an outright open-frontier win
(the “GLM-5 leads on 7 of 9” framing counts wins *or* ties). The Pareto
envelope
(Figure [6](#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ E3 — the cost-quality Pareto. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
per-call cost on a log scale vs. mean rubric score) passes through
GLM-4-9B (free, $`7.31`$), DeepSeek-V4-Flash (\$0.00017, $`9.10`$),
MiniMax-M2.5 (\$0.0009, $`9.44`$), Kimi-K2.5 (\$0.003, $`9.48`$; its
newer sibling K2.6 reaches $`9.54`$ but is cost-dominated by GLM-5), and
GLM-5 (\$0.004/call, $`9.58`$) — the single highest-quality model in the
panel, yet $`{\sim}10`$–$`15\times`$ cheaper than the comparable closed
flagships (claude-opus-4-5 \$0.043/$`9.36`$, claude-opus-4-7
\$0.070/$`9.32`$, gpt-5.4-pro \$0.074/$`9.38`$); the cheaper open
frontier points (MiniMax-M2.5 \$0.0009/$`9.44`$, DeepSeek-V4-Flash
\$0.0002/$`9.10`$) undercut those same flagships
$`{\sim}50`$–$`80\times`$, across $`N{=}49`$ tested models. *Scope
(E3):* emotional-accompaniment evaluation at a 2026-Q1 pricing snapshot,
with open-weight prices from the SiliconFlow aggregator (the invoices we
paid) and closed prices vendor-direct — not a claim that open-weight
dominates the LLM frontier generally. *For a deployment decision:* for a
team deploying emotional-support dialogue specifically, the open-weight
frontier delivers equal-or-indistinguishable quality at one-tenth to
one-eightieth the per-call cost, so the closed-flagship premium buys
essentially nothing on this task — the rational default here is an
open-weight model.

Figure 6: Cost-quality Pareto across $`N{=}49`$ tested models in 8
families. Per-call generation cost (log $`x`$) vs. mean rubric score
across 9 dimensions ($`y`$); the bold red line is the Pareto envelope,
owned end-to-end by open weights. GLM-5 (\$0.004/call, score $`9.58`$)
matches the top closed flagships at $`{\sim}15\times`$ lower cost; the
cheapest open points undercut them $`{\sim}80\times`$. Per-model
coordinates and the per-dimension ledger in
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px7 "E3 — the cost-quality Pareto. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").
Scope: emotional-accompaniment at a 2026-Q1 pricing snapshot.

##### E4 — cross-naturalistic agreement on held-out ESConv.

On 74 real human seeker/supporter conversations the rubric never saw,
four LLM judges from four families agree at $`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$ —
higher than on synthetic data — while we disclose, rather than hide,
that the judge–human floor on this domain is only $`\rho\approx 0.40`$.
The locked rubric is applied to real conversations sampled stratified by
emotion type from ESConv \[[79](#bib.bib30)\], which neither rubric
evolution nor any judge has seen. Canonical claude agrees with all three
non-canonical judges (Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, DeepSeek-V3.2, GLM-5) at
$`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$, and the three non-canonical judges agree among
themselves — without canonical in the loop — at
$`\rho\in[0.618,0.786]`$, ruling out a Qwen-specific shared-bias
reading; all 9 dims clear $`\rho\geq 0.53`$ and 6 of 9 clear
$`\rho\geq 0.70`$. This is replication evidence *among LLM raters*, not
human-rater alignment, which is why we report the judge–human floor
alongside it: the aggregate Spearman between our ensemble and ESConv
participant self-ratings is moderate ($`\rho\approx 0.40`$, $`N{=}64`$,
at or below the documented human–human ceiling $`\rho\approx 0.45`$),
rising monotonically to $`\rho{=}0.62`$ in the top score-bucket —
roughly $`2\times`$ the aggregate — so the rubric agrees with humans
best where ranking high-quality supporter behavior matters most. The
construct-validity gap to human ratings is evidence we have, not a
closed question. *The significance is external validity:* because this
held-out human corpus was generated by neither us nor any model in the
panel, the agreement shows the instrument measures a real property of
supportive dialogue rather than an artifact of our synthetic generation
pipeline — so the E1–E3 findings, all measured on model-generated
conversations, are not a quirk of that distribution.

## 4 Conclusion

Capability scaling does not transfer uniformly to the most human-facing
behaviors — warm restraint above all — and we could only trust that
finding because we built an instrument for when human raters saturate.
Across 49 models, 8 families, and 24 months, advice_restraint is the
universal lowest dimension (mean $`8.28`$, a $`1.3`$-pt gap below
safety), and at gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 it went *backwards* by $`-2.44`$ —
the only advice_restraint transition exceeding $`|\Delta|{=}1.0`$ across
26 and $`5\times`$ the next-largest — a capability–warm-restraint
dissociation we read as the behavioral fingerprint of helpfulness-RLHF
over-optimization (a hypothesis under test, cf. \[[49](#bib.bib90),
[115](#bib.bib113), [99](#bib.bib114)\]). A first direct test points to
suppression over loss: an explicit “listen, don’t solve” instruction
recovers gpt-5 by $`+3.46`$ above gpt-4.1’s default (cross-judge
corroborated). The instrument that measured this substitutes *three
certificates* — reliability, cross-instrument replication, and
historical-footprint calibration — for human-anchor validity, and it
tracked behavioral change *bidirectionally* (it caught both the gpt-5
drop and gpt-5.4’s $`+1.71`$ recovery on the same axis). Beyond the
headline it maps a scoreboard of scaling levers (generation scales;
parameters, MoE width, inference-budget, and reasoning-mode tuning do
not), an open-weight cost-quality Pareto ($`{\sim}10`$–$`80\times`$
*cheaper* at this snapshot), and a cross-naturalistic replication on
held-out human ESConv conversations that neither we nor any panel model
generated.

*Availability:* code, the locked rubric, all judge prompts, and the
4-artifact audit trail will be released upon publication.
````
