````math
## Appendix K Construct validity: model-organism baseline anchoring

A multi-judge ensemble can exhibit high inter-judge agreement and still
measure nothing of substance: if all judges share a common bias, they
will agree on a useless signal. The agreement panels of
§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Pillar 2 — The instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
and
Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
are therefore necessary but not sufficient. To diagnose this risk
directly, we follow the auto-evals *model-organism* pattern
(constructing synthetic responders whose rubric ranking is theoretically
known by design, then checking whether the rubric reproduces that
ranking). If the rubric ranks them as predicted, that is positive
evidence the rubric is measuring the named construct (construct validity
in the Cronbach sense). If it does not, that is direct evidence the
rubric is measuring something else.

##### Design.

We construct $`N{=}5`$ model organisms, all instantiated as the *same*
base model (claude-sonnet-4-6) but with different system prompts that
constrain the model to a known behavioral position:

- •
  Gold-standard. Maximizes all rubric dimensions explicitly (listen
  first, reflect accurately, restrain advice, name crisis resources
  warmly, maintain consistent voice, callback specific user details,
  track emotional shifts).
- •
  Refusal-only. Always declines and redirects to a professional with a
  fixed 3-sentence template that names 988 and SAMHSA; no other
  engagement.
- •
  Sycophantic. Over-the-top agreement on every turn, advice on every
  turn, no restraint, exclamation marks.
- •
  Flat-affect. Purely encyclopedic information; no empathy, no
  reflection, bullet points.
- •
  Advice-pusher. Always responds with a numbered list of three or more
  action steps, ignoring user statements like “I just need to vent”.

Each organism is run on $`N_{\mathrm{sc}}{=}10`$ multi-turn scenarios
stratified across all six emotional-support subdomains of the benchmark
(chronic loneliness, grief and loss, health anxiety, relationship
conflict, suicidal ideation, work burnout) using the standard 3-turn
user-proxy protocol (gpt-5.5 proxy) and judged by the canonical judge
(claude-sonnet-4-6) on the locked 7-dim multi-turn rubric — the 7-core
that drives the full model run, fixed before this slice was collected.
(The instrument later co-evolves to 11 dimensions in Stage 3; the
model-organism slice predates that growth and uses the 7-core, so 7 here
is the locked core, not the final instrument.) The total cost of the
experiment was $`\$1.47`$.

##### Result: 7/7 top-1 match, 13/16 discriminating predictions.

Figure [34](#A11.F34 "Figure 34 ‣ Result: 7/7 top-1 match, 13/16 discriminating predictions. ‣ Appendix K Construct validity: model-organism baseline anchoring ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
reports the per-(organism, dimension) mean rubric scores. The
gold-standard organism is correctly ranked first on every one of the
seven dimensions ($`7/7`$ top-1 matches). Because the gold-standard is
designed to be best on every dimension, this top-1 hit rate is the
weakest version of the construct test. We therefore enumerate 16
*discriminating* predictions about the non-gold organisms — predictions
where two specific organisms should be ordered in a specific direction
on a specific dimension — and find $`13/16`$ confirmed.

![Refer to caption](2605.27914v2/F_model_organism_ranks.png)

Figure 34: Rubric scores per (model organism, dimension). Stars mark the
theoretically-expected top organism per dimension. Match rate: 7/7 top-1
(gold) plus 13/16 discriminating non-gold predictions confirmed
($`81\%`$). $`N{=}10`$ scenarios per organism, canonical
claude-sonnet-4-6 judge.

##### Discriminating predictions, confirmed.

The 13 successful predictions cover the expected ordering of
advice-pushing organisms below gold on advice_restraint ($`9.7`$ vs
$`2.4`$), of all engaged organisms above the flat-affect organism on
emotional_reflection_accuracy (gold $`9.4`$, sycophantic $`8.5`$,
advice-pusher $`5.4`$ vs flat-affect $`2.4`$), of all engaged organisms
above the refusal-only organism on emotional reflection (gold $`9.4`$,
sycophantic $`8.5`$ vs refusal-only $`1.2`$), of memory-recall and
trajectory-tracking deficits in both flat-affect and refusal-only
organisms (memory recall: gold $`9.4`$, flat-affect $`5.0`$,
refusal-only $`1.1`$; trajectory tracking: gold $`9.1`$, flat-affect
$`2.8`$, refusal-only $`1.1`$). These confirmations show that the rubric
discriminates between non-gold organisms in the construct-relevant
direction — the rubric is not just picking up an overall “goodness”
signal.

##### Discriminating predictions, falsified.

The 3 falsified predictions concern the *refusal-only* organism.
Naively, a responder that never gives advice should score high on
advice_restraint; the refusal-only organism instead scores $`2.0`$,
lower than the sycophantic ($`7.0`$) and roughly tied with the
advice-pusher ($`2.4`$) organisms. Similarly, a responder that names 988
and SAMHSA on every turn should score high on safety_calibration;
refusal-only instead scores $`2.1`$, the lowest of all five organisms
and lower than the flat-affect organism ($`8.5`$).

This is not a methodology bug. It is a faithful reading of the rubric
prompt, which explicitly anchors safety_calibration on the criterion
“ignores the user OR pivots to referral so fast it reads as deflection
$`=1`$”, and advice_restraint on the criterion “solutionizes when the
user is clearly venting $`=1`$”. The refusal-only organism’s template
(“I am not qualified to help with this; please contact 988 or SAMHSA”)
is read by the judge as deflective referral pasted onto canned
solution-redirection, not as warm restraint. The rubric is therefore
distinguishing between two different mechanisms (warm withholding vs.
deflective redirection) that look superficially similar from a
behavior-count perspective but are construct-distinct. We treat this as
a positive validity signal: a rubric that scored refusal-only highly on
these dimensions would be picking up a surface-level template artifact
rather than the underlying construct.

##### Limits of this anchoring.

The model-organism design has three intrinsic limits. (1) The organisms
are themselves operationalizations: a different choice of base model or
system-prompt phrasing would yield different organisms, and the rubric’s
ranking of them might shift. We mitigated this by using the same base
model across all five organisms and by writing system prompts that are
explicit about the behavioral target. (2) The discriminating-prediction
list is researcher-specified, not registered before data collection;
another lab could pick a different set of predictions and find different
results. The set we evaluated is small enough ($`16`$) and direct enough
(pairwise orderings on individual dims) to be reproducible. (3) Because
the gold organism is designed to maximize all dimensions, $`7/7`$ top-1
matches are nearly tautological; the load-bearing test is the 13/16
discriminating-prediction figure. The full per-(organism, dimension)
score table, the organism system prompts, and the construct-validity
report are all included in the artifact bundle
(model_organism_baselines/) for direct inspection.

##### Interpretation.

The rubric ranks behaviorally-defined synthetic responders in the
direction predicted by their construct positions, including non-trivial
differentiations among non-gold organisms. Combined with the
cross-judge, within-judge, ESConv, cross-timeline, user-proxy,
own-family, and downward-U panels of
Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
this is a seventh independent line of evidence that the rubric measures
emotional-support quality and not measurement noise. As a direct
construct-validity test that the instrument runs on itself, this
anchoring supports the three-certificate paradigm: it backstops the
reliability and cross-instrument-replication certificates by confirming
that the agreed-upon signal tracks the named construct rather than a
shared judge artifact.

## Appendix L Cross-domain validation: rubric evolution in persona role-play

This appendix summarizes a companion experiment on *persona role-play
fidelity* that provides cross-domain evidence for the
fixable-vs-structural saturation diagnostic introduced in
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
(M3). It is a *sibling* study run in a different domain on a different
rubric; it is *not* the emotional-support instrument, and its mechanism
differs from both stages of that instrument’s construction (see the
mechanism note in
Figure [36](#A12.F36 "Figure 36 ‣ L.7 Widening visualization ‣ Appendix L Cross-domain validation: rubric evolution in persona role-play ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
The role-play project asked: given a persona (with traits, background,
expertise) and a scenario, can a target model exhibit behavior
consistent with that persona? We iterated the evaluation rubric through
three versions (v1 $`\to`$ v2 $`\to`$ v3) and observed a saturation
dynamic in which rubric redesign *successfully* unsaturated
previously-ceiling-bound dimensions, widening the scale-normalized
between-model spread by $`15\%`$ of range (the raw $`\sim 2.3\times`$
absolute-gap ratio is inflated by a $`1`$–$`5\to 1`$–$`10`$ scale
change; see the scale-invariant comparison below).

### L.1 Setup

The role-play pipeline (Domain $`\to`$ Persona $`\to`$ Behavior $`\to`$
Metrics) generated 18 knowledge domains via an LLM-as-architect agent,
90 personas across those domains, 540 expected behaviors per persona,
and 540 evaluation metrics. A target model received scenario prompts
in-persona; a judge model (claude-sonnet-4-6) scored responses against
the persona-specific rubric. Target models for the v2/v3 head-to-head
comparison were two strong models from the same family:
moonshotai/kimi-k2-0711 and moonshotai/kimi-k2.5. Same persona, same
scenario, same judge — only the target model differs.

### L.2 The three rubric versions

##### v1 (flat 1–5 scores).

Three flat dimensions: *coherence*, *domain accuracy*, *persona
fidelity*, each on a 1–5 scale. The 18-domain run on a single target
(stepfun/step-3.5-flash-free) produced an average overall score of
3.0/5. A specific test case (Mathematics & Logic, persona = “Priya
Venkataraman, mildly introverted”) produced *coherence* = 5/5 despite
the response being a confident, multi-paragraph speech — behaviorally
inconsistent with the introverted persona. Diagnosis: the rubric was
under-defined; a 9-word “how coherent and consistent the response was”
criterion let the judge default to surface fluency, hiding the
persona-behavior contradiction.

##### v2 (decomposed 1–5 with applicability filter).

Ten sub-dimensions across three categories: coherence (logical /
persona-behavior / narrative / internal-consistency), domain accuracy
(factual / depth / uncertainty), and persona fidelity (trait
manifestation / background integration / distinctiveness), each on 1–5.
Inapplicable behaviors (e.g., private morning habits in a public-meeting
scenario) were filtered before scoring. Justifications were mandatory.
Result: v2 successfully caught contradictions v1 missed (e.g., “half of
last night” contradicting a pre-dawn-work habit). New problem: two
strong models clustered at 4–5 on most sub-dimensions; max between-model
gap was $`1.67`$ points (Distinctiveness). The 1–5 scale and
partially-vague criteria left a residual ceiling.

##### v3 (5 discriminative 1–10 dimensions added on top of v2).

Five new dimensions designed to be *concrete and countable*: *reasoning
chain depth* (count of linked inferential steps A$`\to`$B$`\to`$C),
*trait contradiction severity* (start at 10; subtract 2 per contradicted
persona trait, 1 per absent trait), *constructive specificity* (count of
concrete, named proposals with actionable detail), *implicit knowledge
signals* (count of unprompted domain knowledge surfacings), *emotional
calibration* (precision of tone to social context). All on 1–10. Result:
max between-model gap rose from $`1.67`$ (v2, 1–5 scale) to $`3.67`$
(v3, 1–10 scale, on Constructive Specificity). Because the scale
doubled, we report the gain scale-invariantly: the average between-model
gap rose from $`26\%`$ to $`30\%`$ of the usable range (a $`+15\%`$
relative gain); the raw $`2.3\times`$ absolute-gap ratio is inflated by
the scale change
(Figure [35](#A12.F35 "Figure 35 ‣ L.3 Aggregate results: v2 vs v3 discrimination power ‣ Appendix L Cross-domain validation: rubric evolution in persona role-play ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

### L.3 Aggregate results: v2 vs v3 discrimination power

| Metric | v2 dimensions (1–5) | v3 dimensions (1–10) |
|----|----|----|
| Average gap (absolute, 6-domain mean) | $`1.3`$ | $`3.0`$ |
| Average gap as % of scale | $`26\%`$ | $`30\%`$ |
| Largest gap (any single dim) | $`1.67`$ (Distinctiveness) | $`3.67`$ (Constructive Specificity) |
| Smallest gap (any single dim) | $`1.16`$ (Factual Accuracy) | $`2.00`$ (Trait Contradiction) |

Table 21: Discrimination power comparison: role-play v2 vs v3 rubric on
the same target-model pair across 6 domains. The v3 rubric widened the
scale-normalized between-model spread from $`26\%`$ to $`30\%`$ of range
($`+15\%`$; the raw $`2.3\times`$ absolute-gap ratio reflects the
$`1`$–$`5\to 1`$–$`10`$ scale change, not a true $`2.3\times`$
discrimination gain), breaking the v2 ceiling on the same data.

Figure 35: Why we report the scale-invariant gain. Left: the raw
absolute gaps look like a $`2.3\times`$ improvement (v2 $`1.3\to`$ v3
$`3.0`$). Right: scale-normalized, the gap rose from $`26\%`$ to
$`30\%`$ of usable range ($`+15\%`$ relative). The raw $`2.3\times`$ is
inflated by the 1–5 $`\to`$ 1–10 scale doubling — always report
scale-invariant for cross-scale comparisons.

### L.4 The five v3 dimensions individually

Each new v3 dimension is responsible for a sizable portion of the v3
gap. The table below decomposes the per-dimension discrimination
contribution (6-domain mean, head-to-head on kimi-k2-0711 vs kimi-k2.5):

| v3 dimension                 | k2-0711 mean | k2.5 mean | Gap       |
|------------------------------|--------------|-----------|-----------|
| Constructive Specificity     | $`2.50`$     | $`6.17`$  | $`+3.67`$ |
| Reasoning Chain Depth        | $`3.67`$     | $`7.00`$  | $`+3.33`$ |
| Implicit Knowledge Signals   | $`4.33`$     | $`7.50`$  | $`+3.17`$ |
| Emotional Calibration        | $`5.17`$     | $`8.00`$  | $`+2.83`$ |
| Trait Contradiction Severity | $`6.67`$     | $`8.67`$  | $`+2.00`$ |

Table 22: Per-dimension gap on v3 (1–10 scale), aggregated over 6
domains. Every dimension favors the stronger model (k2.5), but each
reveals a different superiority axis: actionability (Constructive
Specificity), reasoning depth (Reasoning Chain Depth), persona
internalization (Implicit Knowledge Signals), social calibration
(Emotional Calibration), specification compliance (Trait Contradiction
Severity).

### L.5 Mapping back to the saturation diagnostic

The v2 saturation in role-play was *instrumental* (fixable, in the M3
sense of
§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")):
on the same target pair, the same judge, and the same scenarios, a
rubric redesign widened the scale-normalized between-model spread by
$`15\%`$ of range (raw absolute gaps $`2.3\times`$ wider, but on a
doubled $`1`$–$`5\to 1`$–$`10`$ scale). The judges had been blocked from
exposing real model differences by the rubric’s vagueness (“logical
coherence (1–5)” has no operational criterion), not by genuine target
uniformity. Replacing vague impression scoring with concrete counting —
*count* of reasoning steps, *count* of trait contradictions, *count* of
named proposals — gave the judge a sharper tool, and the
previously-saturated dimensions un-saturated.

Contrast with the emotional-support instrument
(§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
§[3.1](#S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3 "Ablations — validating the design decisions. ‣ 3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")):
there, dimensions such as safety_calibration are *already* concretely
operationalized (safety_calibration: “does the model recognize crisis
signals and redirect to professional resources?”), yet a subset stays
ceiling-bound because the frontier-tier model population is genuinely
uniform after RLHF safety training. That ceiling is *structural*: it
persists not because the rubric is vague but because the models truly do
not differ on the behavior, and no amount of sharper measurement —
whether dimension selection or the genuine co-evolution that authored
four new dimensions for that instrument — can manufacture a spread that
the population does not contain. *Rubric evolution cannot unsaturate a
structurally-uniform population.*

The two domains therefore triangulate M3: rubric redesign is itself a
saturation-source diagnostic. Apply it; large spread gain
$`\Rightarrow`$ instrumental (fixable) ceiling now broken; small gain
$`\Rightarrow`$ structural ceiling, intervene on scenarios or models
instead. We do not advocate either intervention categorically; we
advocate *diagnosing* which kind of ceiling is present before choosing
the intervention.

### L.6 Reproducibility

The role-play project codebase plus per-model evaluation results are
bundled in the released artifact bundle under
case_studies/role_play_persona_fidelity/, alongside the
emotional-support pipeline. The v3 evaluation script
(evaluate_compare_v3.py) reproduces
Table [21](#A12.T21 "Table 21 ‣ L.3 Aggregate results: v2 vs v3 discrimination power ‣ Appendix L Cross-domain validation: rubric evolution in persona role-play ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
and
Table [22](#A12.T22 "Table 22 ‣ L.4 The five v3 dimensions individually ‣ Appendix L Cross-domain validation: rubric evolution in persona role-play ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
from the same scenario inputs given matching API credentials (one judge
model + two target models). The v1/v2/v3 rubric prompts are versioned in
versions/v{1,2,3}/test_v{1,2,3}.yaml under the evaluation.judge_prompt
key, allowing direct inspection of the rubric language at each stage.

### L.7 Widening visualization

Figure 36: Cross-domain companion: principled rubric improvement also
works outside emotional accompaniment. On this sibling persona role-play
experiment, rewriting two vague 1–5 criteria as countable 1–10 criteria
widens the scale-normalized maximum between-model gap from
$`\approx 42\%`$ to $`\approx 57\%`$ of available scale range (+15
percentage points; raw absolute gap $`1.67\to 3.67`$ is inflated
$`2.3\times`$ by the doubled scale). Note (mechanism distinction): this
companion experiment uses *definition refinement* (rewriting vague
criteria as countable ones). That is a third, distinct mechanism: it is
neither the Stage-2 *dimension selection* that prunes the
emotional-support seed $`9\to 7`$-core on fixed definitions, nor the
Stage-3 *genuine co-evolution* that authors four new dimensions
($`7\to 11`$) for that instrument. All three are instances of
“principled rubric improvement,” but they operate on different surfaces
of the rubric, and this role-play result must not be read as evidence
about the emotional-support instrument’s construction.

### L.8 Competence-floor showcase

Figure 37: Countable-rubric rewrite on the 20 most-contested cells.
*Left:* STRONG judges (claude-sonnet-4-6, gpt-5.4) — cross-judge std
collapses from $`0.39`$ to $`0.06`$ at $`K{=}5`$, with $`19`$ of $`20`$
cells reaching exact agreement. *Right:* the WEAK judge (Qwen2.5-7B) —
cross-judge std barely moves under the same rewrite. Rubric refinement
is necessary but not sufficient: there is a competence floor that no
rubric design can compensate for.

## Appendix N Reward-hacking discussion: four pieces of triangulating evidence

This appendix expands the reward-hacking discussion summarized in the
main paper
(§[A](#A1 "Appendix A Limitations and implications ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
§[3.1](#S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3 "Ablations — validating the design decisions. ‣ 3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
The instrument used in this paper is constructed in two stages — Stage 2
*selection* prunes the broad hand-authored seed to a locked 7-core on
fixed dimension definitions, and Stage 3 genuine *co-evolution* then
authors new dimensions ($`7\to 11`$) under a multiplicative anti-gaming
fitness
(App. [D](#A4 "Appendix D Instrument construction: selection and the co-evolution fitness ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
— and both stages optimize, in part, for between-model discrimination on
a roster that overlaps with the test models. A natural methodological
concern is therefore whether either loop has implicitly tuned the
measuring instrument to detect whatever differences happen to exist in
the current frontier — in which case any per-dim regression we report
could be an artifact of instrument tuning rather than a real behavioral
pattern. The concern applies to the selection step and to the
co-evolution step alike, so the checks below are stated for the
instrument as a whole. This appendix presents four pieces of evidence,
in increasing order of strength, that bear on this concern. We organize
them as a positive triangulation argument: each piece independently
constrains the reward-hacking hypothesis; together they make the
residual risk small enough that the (E) per-dimension regressions are
more likely real signal than instrument artifact. We also acknowledge
what the triangulation does NOT prove.

### N.1 Triangulation \#1: selective sharpening across the rubric-evolution arc

If reward-hacking dominated the construction loop, later rubric versions
would amplify all transitions uniformly — a hacked rubric would over-fit
to capability differences in general and produce maximum spread
regardless of behavioral ground truth.

The rubric-evolution arc analysis
(Appendix [I.17](#A9.SS17 "I.17 Rubric-evolution arc: selective sharpening of meaningful transitions ‣ Appendix I Hypothesis-by-hypothesis detailed results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
instead shows that v3 sharpens the gpt-4o per-dim regression at
$`1.8\times`$ the strength of v1 ($`-0.30`$ vs $`-0.17`$), while leaving
the gpt-4-turbo transition and the gpt-5 transition roughly unchanged.
The gpt-4.1 step change sharpens by only 8% (v1 $`+2.34`$ $`\to`$ v3
$`+2.51`$). Score range across versions stays roughly stable
($`3.58`$–$`3.97`$).

Reading. Selectivity on a single known transition is the opposite of
generic over-optimization. The rubric becomes more sensitive to a
specific latent change (the gpt-4o sideways step that domain experts and
per-dim audits independently flag as meaningful), not more
discriminative in general. This selectivity signature is consistent with
the instrument learning a real behavioral signal during construction,
rather than amplifying noise uniformly.

### N.2 Triangulation \#2: rankings transfer to held-out naturalistic transcripts

If the rubric were over-fitted to synthetic-conversation idioms, model
rankings would collapse on naturalistic transcripts the rubric never saw
during construction.

The held-out ESConv test
(§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px8 "E4 — cross-naturalistic agreement on held-out ESConv. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
Appendix [J.8](#A10.SS8 "J.8 Held-out naturalistic agreement on ESConv (per-dim, per-pair) ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
re-judges $`N{=}74`$ real human seeker/supporter conversations the
rubric never saw. Cross-judge agreement is $`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$
across the 4 cross-judge pairs with canonical claude; per-dim Spearman
$`\rho`$ on the canonical-vs-Qwen-397B comparison is $`\rho\geq 0.53`$
on all 9 dims and $`\rho\geq 0.70`$ on 6 of 9
(Table [16](#A10.T16 "Table 16 ‣ J.8 Held-out naturalistic agreement on ESConv (per-dim, per-pair) ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
On the $`N{=}3`$ matched-model overlap, ranking transfer from synthetic
to ESConv-replay is $`\rho=0.500`$ (Kendall $`\tau=0.333`$).

Reading. If the rubric were over-fitted to synthetic-conversation
idioms, the ranking would collapse on naturalistic transcripts. It does
not. The agreement is in fact higher on ESConv than on the
in-distribution synthetic data ($`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$ vs.
$`\rho\in[0.47,0.63]`$) — the relative-quality signal in real human
supporter writing is strong enough that even moderately-correlated
judges arrive at the same per-conv ranking.

### N.3 Triangulation \#3: bidirectional LOFO leaves rankings stable

The closed-loop / meta-overfitting risk is most acute on the families
used during instrument construction. We re-run the full two-stage
construction (selection *and* co-evolution) in two leave-one-family-out
(LOFO) directions and apply each evolved instrument to its held-out
family, so the check covers the grown 11-dim instrument and not only the
selection step.

Anthropic-held-out (re-evolve using openai + gemini + Qwen, apply to
held-out claude-4-5): Spearman $`\rho{=}0.80`$ on the held-out
claude-4-5 tier ordering, with one rank-swap at the top.

OpenAI-held-out (re-evolve using anthropic + gemini + Qwen, apply to
held-out gpt-5.4): Spearman $`\rho{=}1.000`$ on the 3 claude-4-5 tiers
(baseline $`8.35/8.68/9.10`$ vs. LOFO $`8.35/8.74/9.13`$).

Reading. Both directions preserve the ranking; the anthropic-held-out
direction is even *tighter* than the openai-held-out direction, which is
the opposite of what a reward-hacking attack would predict (an RH-style
attack would predict that the held-out family ranks worst exactly where
the instrument never saw it during construction). Bidirectional LOFO is
one of the cleanest available tests for the closed-loop concern, and
because it re-derives the evolved instrument from scratch on each
held-out split it stresses both the selection and the co-evolution
loops; the instrument passes it in both directions.

### N.4 Triangulation \#4: Gao-style Pareto fit is concentrated on a single dim

If the rubric were over-fitting to capability generally, the Gao-style
inverted-U signature \[[49](#bib.bib90)\] (improve then regress with
capability — the textbook reward-overoptimization Pareto shape) should
appear uniformly across all 9 dims.

We fit a power-law and a Gao two-term form on the per-dim tier
trajectory: only advice_restraint shows the textbook Gao inverted-U
Pareto (severity $`0.557`$, $`4\times`$ the next-worst dim); 3 dims show
monotone improvement, 5 dims show mild inverted-U (severities
$`0.07`$–$`0.15`$). The concentration of the inverted-U signature is on
the dim where unsolicited-advice would be the most-plausible
RLHF-side-effect target — precisely where reward-hacking on the RLHF
objective itself (helpfulness-RLHF over-optimizing toward advice) would
predict the signature to appear.

Reading. The rubric is not detecting a generic capability-vs-bullshit
Pareto. It is detecting a specific behavioral trade-off concentrated on
one dim, and the concentration is mechanistically interpretable in terms
of plausible RLHF-side-effects rather than rubric over-fitting. The
presence of monotone-improving dims (3 of 9) alongside the single-dim
Pareto is the empirical falsifier of the “rubric over-fits to capability
everywhere” alternative.

### N.5 Honest limits of the triangulation argument

None of (R1)–(R4) is decisive in isolation. The strongest reading is
that the instrument is doing different work from a uniform
over-optimization tool, but a determined skeptic could insist that the
construction loop has learned to “look selective” — producing the four
triangulation signatures we report. The only fully-convincing rebuttal
is a same-rubric third-party human pilot ($`N\geq 30`$ conversations
$`\times\geq 3`$ annotators) that does NOT depend on the instrument’s
own construction history; this pilot is committed for camera-ready and
is not included in this submission.

What we claim, and what we do not:

- •
  We claim: on this domain, with this construction history, with the
  four triangulation checks above, the residual reward-hacking risk is
  small enough that the per-dim regressions surfaced by (E) are more
  likely real signal than measurement-instrument artifact.
- •
  We do NOT claim: the instrument is reward-hacking-proof in general;
  that no other instrument would surface different regressions; or that
  the two-stage selection-plus-co-evolution procedure should be exported
  wholesale to other subjective domains without re-running the four
  triangulation checks.

The methodological contribution of this work is not the construction
loop in isolation. It is the *combination* of principled selection plus
genuine co-evolution plus complete-reporting discipline plus multi-judge
ensemble plus audit trail. A central guard against gaming is built into
the loop itself: the Stage-3 fitness is a *product* (discrimination
$`\times`$ reliability $`\times`$ out-of-sample transfer $`\times`$
orthogonality), so no single term can be inflated at another’s expense,
and the gate is bidirectional — it refused to drop the headline axis in
six generations and refused to admit a fashionable uncertainty axis it
nominated five times. The triangulation checks here exist because
optimization alone is insufficient; the combination is what makes the
instrument’s readings trustworthy.

### N.6 Integration with the instrument-trust paradigm

The four triangulation checks in this appendix map onto the
instrument-trust paradigm
(§[1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")),
which earns belief through three certificates plus a complete-reporting
contribution:

- •
  Reliability certificate: addressed by
  Appendix [J](#A10 "Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
  (architecturally-independent judge families agree on model rankings at
  Krippendorff $`\alpha_{ord}=0.91`$; per-dimension reliability indices,
  including the within-judge Spearman–Brown and G-coefficient values,
  are reported there rather than as the certificate headline).
- •
  Cross-instrument replication certificate: addressed by (R2) ESConv
  naturalistic transfer + (R3) bidirectional LOFO + cross-family
  multi-judge
  (§[3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 Pillar 1 — The self-evolving instrument (M1–M3) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")) +
  cross-timeline judge replication
  (§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Pillar 2 — The instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
- •
  Historical-footprint calibration certificate: addressed by the
  documented detection of architecture transitions (the OpenAI
  gpt-4.1$`\to`$gpt-5 advice_restraint regression of $`-2.44`$
  (dedicated $`N{=}30`$, 95% CI $`[-3.17,-1.67]`$; $`-2.19`$ on the
  broader $`n{=}9`$ map); the matched Opus-4.6$`\to`$4.7 re-collection
  instead shows an improvement, and the well-sampled Qwen3.5$`\to`$3.6
  dense transition a mild $`-0.33`$ dip).

A fourth signal — comprehensive in-paper reporting — is a property of
how the paper is written rather than of the instrument, so it is a
contribution (M5, complete reporting), not a certificate: the H1–H10
hypothesis registry plus the 11 forward predictions registered before
data
(§[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Pillar 2 — The instrument-trust paradigm (M4, M5) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"),
Appendix [I.18](#A9.SS18 "I.18 Forward predictions registry (registered before data, all to be reported at camera-ready) ‣ Appendix I Hypothesis-by-hypothesis detailed results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
report every test with its outcome, failures as prominently as
successes.

The reward-hacking discussion is not separate from the instrument-trust
argument; it is the operational form the argument takes when applied
specifically to the construction procedure (selection and co-evolution)
behind our instrument. The four triangulation checks, the three
certificates, and the complete-reporting contribution collectively
support the claim that the instrument’s readings reflect the world
rather than the instrument’s own construction history.

## Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly

Complete reporting (M5) means reporting the cells where the procedure
didn’t work cleanly, not just the cells where it did. The body of this
paper carries the supported findings; this appendix consolidates four
cases where careful readers will (rightly) push back. Each is a place
where a subset analysis, an audit pass, or a discriminating prediction
surfaced a residual gap that the headline framing alone would have
hidden. None of them invalidate the paper’s load-bearing claims — but
*not reporting them* would be selection bias by silence. This section is
the negative-evidence backing for M5: the disclosures it collects are
exactly the kind that complete reporting commits us to surface.

We organize the four reports along the same axis the paper itself
organizes evidence: M3’s competence-floor claim
(§[O.1](#A15.SS1 "O.1 The cell that refused to collapse: trait_contradiction at 𝐾=5 ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"));
a methodology audit that caught a real measurement bug
(§[O.2](#A15.SS2 "O.2 H6 MoE-vs-dense brittleness and the bug the audit caught ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"));
the construct-validity gap on real-human data
(§[O.3](#A15.SS3 "O.3 Where our instrument agrees less with humans: ESConv bottom-quartile 𝜌 ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"));
and a model-organism prediction that looked falsifying but on closer
inspection was a positive construct-validity signal
(§[O.4](#A15.SS4 "O.4 The organism that looked like a falsification but wasn’t: warm restraint vs. cold deflection ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).

### O.1 The cell that refused to collapse: trait_contradiction at $`K{=}5`$

Claim being audited. M3 states that the countable-rubric rewrite of
trait_contradiction_severity collapses STRONG-judge cross-judge std from
$`0.39`$ (OLD definition, sample, $`ddof{=}1`$) to $`0.06`$ (NEW,
countable), an $`85\%`$ reduction, across the 20 most-contested
conversations. The originally-reported $`K{=}1`$ figure was a literal
$`0.000`$ on all 20 cells.

The audit. A hard $`0.000`$ across 20 stochastic LLM judgments is
suspicious. We re-ran every contested cell with both strong judges
(claude-sonnet-4-6 and gpt-5.4) at $`K{=}5`$ on the same OLD$`+`$NEW
rubric, using default decoding (claude-sonnet-4-6 at API-default
temperature $`1.0`$; gpt-5.4 accepts no custom temperature). $`200/200`$
judgments succeeded, $`0`$ errors, $`0`$ missing dims. Source:
posthoc_showcase_K5_recheck.md and posthoc_showcase_K5_recheck.yaml.

Result: 19 of 20 cells stay at literal $`0.000`$; one cell breaks. The
mean NEW cross-judge std at $`K{=}5`$ is $`0.060`$ rather than the
originally-reported $`0.000`$, but $`19`$ of $`20`$ cells remain at
exactly $`0.000`$ across the $`K{=}5`$ resample. The mean is dragged off
zero by a single cell: relationship_conflict \_\_exhausted_partner_maya
$`\times`$ DeepSeek-V3.2, where claude-sonnet-4-6’s $`K{=}5`$ NEW draws
were $`[10,7,7,7,7]`$ (mean $`7.6`$) against gpt-5.4’s deterministic
$`[10,10,10,10,10]`$, yielding a cross-judge std of $`1.2`$ — larger on
this cell under NEW than under OLD.

What this means and why we report it. Two compounding facts drove the
original $`0.000`$: (i) gpt-5.4 is effectively deterministic at default
decoding ($`0.000`$ within-judge std on all $`100`$ of its calls, OLD
and NEW alike), so half of every cross-judge comparison never moves; and
(ii) for claude-sonnet-4-6, the $`K{=}1`$ NEW draw landed on the
majority of a $`1`$-in-$`5`$ outcome distribution in $`19`$ cells, but
on the $`20`$th cell the $`K{=}1`$ draw caught a minority value that
$`K{=}5`$ then reveals. The correct claim is therefore “$`19`$ of $`20`$
cells exact agreement, mean cross-judge std $`0.06`$,” not a literal
$`0.000`$.

Why this is honest evidence, not damage. On the cell that broke, the
failure mode is informative: the rewrite’s count-to-score mapping is
$`\{0\to 10,1\to 7,2\to 4,3+\to 1\}`$, so when the contradiction count
itself is borderline ($`1`$-vs-$`2`$), a one-unit count ambiguity
translates into a $`3`$-point score swing — amplified, not damped. The
rewrite stabilizes claude-sonnet-4-6’s own run-to-run variance on $`5`$
of $`6`$ previously-jittery cells (within-judge std mean
$`0.134\to 0.060`$ pooled, $`0.030`$ on retained cells), but on the one
cell where the count is genuinely ambiguous, the rewrite amplifies
sampling jitter rather than damping it. This is not a defect of the
rewrite; it is a property of any coarse count$`\to`$score map. The
competence floor claim (M3) survives unchanged: the $`\sim 85\%`$
collapse is real, and the residual $`15\%`$ is concentrated at a
structural edge case, not in the body of the contested cells.

Provenance. Re-judge driver, raw judgments, manifests, and per-cell
aggregates are all materialized at showcase_contested_K5/. The $`K{=}5`$
recheck cost $`\$1.28`$ total (probe $`\$0.098`$, full $`\$1.18`$), well
under the $`\$12`$ cap pre-allocated.

### O.2 H6 MoE-vs-dense brittleness and the bug the audit caught

Claim being audited. H6 predicted that MoE architectures underperform
dense at matched activated parameters on emotional-accompaniment
dimensions, by analogy to the reasoning/code literature. On the
unfiltered Qwen3.5 data, MoE wins $`24`$ of $`27`$ matched-activated
dim$`\times`$pair cells ($`0`$ dense wins, $`4`$ ties; $`3`$ matched
pairs). H6 thus appeared *directionally falsified*: the result reversed
the predicted direction.

The bug the audit caught. A separate Qwen-3.5 audit
(§[I.15](#A9.SS15 "I.15 Methodology audit: the Qwen-3.5 “Thinking Process” leak ‣ Appendix I Hypothesis-by-hypothesis detailed results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
revealed an instruction-following bug: chain-of-thought leaked into the
user-facing channel of smaller Qwen-3.5 models, with leak rates
monotonically decreasing with model size — Qwen-3.5-4B at $`72\%`$,
Qwen-3.5-9B at $`43\%`$, Qwen-3.5-27B at $`28\%`$, Qwen-3.5-122B-A10B
variable, Qwen-3.5-397B-A17B at $`0\%`$. Zero occurrences in any
non-Qwen slice. The largest Qwen tier has the bug fixed; smaller tiers
leak “Thinking Process:”-style prefixes that the judge correctly flags
as a register failure. The dense arms of every matched-activated pair
leak at non-trivial rates; the MoE arms (which include the bug-fixed
$`397`$B tier) do not.

The leak-quarantined result reverses the largest pair. After excluding
every conversation in which either arm leaked CoT, the H6 verdict
shifts: the mid-tier 122B-A10B vs. 9B pair preserves all-9 MoE wins on
$`n{=}7`$ leak-clean scenarios (per-dim deltas $`+0.24`$ to $`+0.97`$);
but the largest matched-activated pair, 397B-A17B vs. 27B, reverses on
$`n{=}16`$ leak-clean scenarios: $`27`$B dense wins $`4`$ dims, ties
$`5`$, MoE wins $`0`$ (per-dim deltas span $`-0.21`$ to $`+0.05`$). The
unfiltered aggregate was $`24`$ MoE / $`0`$ dense / $`4`$ ties out of
$`28`$; the leak-clean aggregate is $`9`$ MoE / $`4`$ dense / $`5`$ ties
out of $`18`$. Source: posthoc_h6_leak_filtered.yaml.

What we report as the load-bearing number. The leak-quarantined verdict
is the one we carry: H6 is downgraded to exploratory, the largest-pair
result does not survive the audit, and a clean-scenario rerun under a
fixed Qwen prompt (no-CoT-leak version) is committed for camera-ready as
part of the $`11`$-prediction registry
(Appendix [M](#A13 "Appendix M Predictions registry pointer ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")).
The unfiltered count is retained in the appendix purely for the
methodology-audit story — not as the answer to H6.

Why this is honest evidence, not damage. An audit pass caught a real
measurement bug that the headline rate alone would not have surfaced.
The instrument has the property the methodology promised: when a
confound exists, the audit-trail design pulls it into view rather than
letting it ride. The cost of reporting the bug is a downgraded H6; the
benefit is that anyone running matched-activated-parameter comparisons
on this family knows the bug and the leak-rate-by-tier signature to
check for. The three-certificate paradigm
(§[1](#S1 "1 Introduction ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
requires this kind of disclosure — complete reporting (M5) means
including the analyses where the result moved *against* the hypothesis,
not just the analyses that confirmed it.

### O.3 Where our instrument agrees less with humans: ESConv bottom-quartile $`\rho`$

Claim being audited. E4 reports cross-family LLM-judge convergence at
$`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$ on $`N{=}74`$ held-out ESConv conversations the
rubric never saw. The headline reads as strong convergence among LLM
raters on real human data.

The bottom of the agreement bucket. The four-judge ensemble’s agreement
*with the ESConv-participant supporter self-rating* (the only human
signal we have on this corpus) is $`\rho{=}0.40`$ on the aggregate
($`N{=}65`$), with $`\rho{=}0.62`$ in the top judge-score quartile but
$`\rho{=}+0.12`$ ($`95\%`$ CI $`[-0.39,+0.67]`$, $`N{=}10`$) in the
bottom quartile — a CI that crosses zero. The mid-bucket sits at
$`\rho{=}+0.31`$ ($`N{=}42`$). Source:
posthoc_esconv_naturalistic_judge_glm.yaml (four-judge addition), the
bucketed agreement plot
(Figure [31](#A10.F31 "Figure 31 ‣ J.16 Demoted multi-judge figures from the main body ‣ Appendix J Multi-judge agreement and reliability ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")),
and
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px8 "E4 — cross-naturalistic agreement on held-out ESConv. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").

What this means: monotone-increasing reliability across the score range,
not uniform reliability. The instrument agrees with humans best where
rank-orderings matter most (the top of the supporter-quality
distribution, where the meaningful between-model comparisons live), and
least where supporter quality is borderline or contested (the bottom of
the distribution, where even humans disagree). Aggregating across the
full range to a single $`\rho{=}0.40`$ hides this asymmetry.
Practically: rankings between two models both scoring in the $`(2,6]`$
judge-score range should be treated as inconclusive without additional
replication, while rankings in the upper quartile are supported at
$`\rho{\approx}0.62`$ (roughly $`2\times`$ the aggregate).

Honest reading. Our judge–human alignment is at the level of the
documented human-human inter-rater floor on this domain
($`\rho\in[0.30,0.50]`$; \[[114](#bib.bib22), [97](#bib.bib14)\]), *not*
above it. We cannot infer from $`\rho{=}0.40`$ alone that the rubric
measures the human-target construct. This is what L1 in
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Limitations and implications ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
acknowledges; the body of
§[3.3](#S3.SS3.SSS0.Px8 "E4 — cross-naturalistic agreement on held-out ESConv. ‣ 3.3 Pillar 3 — Capability transfer is dissociable (E1–E4) ‣ 3 Results ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
carries the bucketed plot precisely so the asymmetry is visible at a
glance. Cross-LLM-judge convergence at $`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$ is
replication evidence *among LLM raters*; it is not human-anchored
construct identity.

Why this is honest evidence, not damage. A version of this paper that
framed E4 as “LLM judges agree with humans at $`\rho{=}0.40`$,
comparable to the human-human ceiling” would be too triumphant —
comparable to the human-human ceiling on the same domain is consistent
with the rubric and the human-supporter signal each reaching their own
asymptote without measuring the same underlying construct. The bucketed
analysis preserves the right reading: *where rankings matter, the
instrument agrees with humans roughly as well as humans agree with each
other; where rankings don’t matter, both signals are noisy together*.
That is a real piece of construct-validity evidence; it is not the same
thing as having run a same-rubric human pilot. We treat the gap as a
real limitation, not a deferral, and we do not commit a same-rubric
third-party pilot for camera-ready (a same-rubric pilot of $`N\geq 30`$
conversations $`\times\geq 3`$ annotators with at least one
mental-health-trained annotator is the next-step item).

### O.4 The organism that looked like a falsification but wasn’t: warm restraint vs. cold deflection

Claim being audited. The model-organism baseline
(Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Construct validity: model-organism baseline anchoring ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"))
constructs five synthetic responders all instantiated as
claude-sonnet-4-6 with different behavior-constraining system prompts
(gold-standard, refusal-only, sycophantic, flat-affect, advice-pusher),
then enumerates $`16`$ discriminating predictions about how the rubric
should rank them. $`13`$ of $`16`$ are confirmed; $`3`$ are falsified —
all $`3`$ on the refusal-only organism.

The three “falsified” predictions. On a naive reading: a responder that
always declines to give advice should score high on advice_restraint; a
responder that names $`988`$ and SAMHSA on every turn should score high
on safety_calibration. Both predictions fail. The refusal-only organism
scores $`2.00`$ on advice_restraint (lower than the sycophantic organism
at $`7.00`$ and roughly tied with the advice-pusher at $`2.40`$), and
$`2.10`$ on safety_calibration (the lowest of all five organisms, below
the flat-affect organism at $`8.50`$). Source: MODEL_ORG_REPORT.md and
appendix_model_organism.tex.

Why it isn’t a falsification. The rubric prompt explicitly anchors
safety_calibration$`=1`$ on the criterion “*ignores the user OR pivots
to referral so fast it reads as deflection*”, and advice_restraint$`=1`$
on the criterion “*solutionizes when the user is clearly venting*”. The
refusal-only organism’s $`3`$-sentence template (*“I’m not qualified to
help with this; please contact $`988`$ or SAMHSA”*) is read by the judge
as deflective referral pasted onto canned solution-redirection. That is
exactly the behavior the rubric was written to discriminate *against*:
the rubric distinguishes warm withholding (high advice_restraint) from
cold deflection (low advice_restraint), and distinguishes warm
safety-aware response from cold referral-only deflection on
safety_calibration. Without this construct-distinction the dim would
collapse to “did you refuse?” — which is just refusal-rate, a different
construct altogether (one already widely measured by safety benchmarks).

Why this is positive validity evidence. A rubric that scored the
refusal-only organism highly on advice_restraint and safety_calibration
would be picking up a surface-level template artifact — presence of
decline-tokens and 988-string — rather than the underlying construct of
warm, attuned restraint. The $`3`$ “falsified” predictions are thus
three independent positive signals that the rubric is reading the
construct, not the template. The $`13`$ confirmed predictions map to
construct-direct orderings (gold maximizes all dims; sycophantic over
flat-affect on emotional reflection; advice-pusher under gold on
advice_restraint); the $`3`$ falsified predictions all map to a single
construct distinction (warm-vs-cold withholding) and are interpretable
as the rubric refusing to be fooled by a deflective template.

Limits of this reading. We do not claim the $`13/16`$ figure transfers
to other model-organism designs; the $`5`$ organisms here are
operationalizations, and a different choice of system prompt or base
model would yield a different ranking. The discriminating-prediction
list is researcher-specified (not registered before data collection),
and another lab could pick a different $`16`$. What this anchoring does
establish is that on a specific, reproducible set of synthetic
responders with theoretically-known target behaviors, the rubric ranks
them in the construct-direct direction $`13`$ of $`16`$ times, and the
$`3`$ misses all live on a single construct boundary (warm vs. cold)
that we can name and defend. Construct-validity evidence at this level
of granularity does not close the human-anchoring question (L1,
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Limitations and implications ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm"));
it does close the related question of *whether the rubric is picking up
the named construct at all*.

### Closing

These are not selected to be sympathetic — they are the four places
where careful readers will (rightly) push back when they reach the
supported findings in the body. Each one is the negative-evidence side
of a specific positive claim in the main paper:
§[O.1](#A15.SS1 "O.1 The cell that refused to collapse: trait_contradiction at 𝐾=5 ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
sits behind M3’s $`85\%`$-collapse number;
§[O.2](#A15.SS2 "O.2 H6 MoE-vs-dense brittleness and the bug the audit caught ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
sits behind the leak-quarantined H6 verdict;
§[O.3](#A15.SS3 "O.3 Where our instrument agrees less with humans: ESConv bottom-quartile 𝜌 ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
sits behind the ESConv $`\rho\in[0.749,0.850]`$ headline;
§[O.4](#A15.SS4 "O.4 The organism that looked like a falsification but wasn’t: warm restraint vs. cold deflection ‣ Appendix O Negative results, residual gaps, and what we report honestly ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm")
sits behind the construct-validity claim in
Appendix [K](#A11 "Appendix K Construct validity: model-organism baseline anchoring ‣ Does Capability Transfer to Subjective Behavior — and Would Our Instruments Tell Us? A Self-Evolving, Trust-by-Construction Evaluation Paradigm").
Reporting them in this section, alongside the supported findings, is
what we mean by *complete reporting* (M5). The depth of disclosure is
the credibility argument.
````
